## 60. An Aggregate Budget for Pointer Validation Under the Retention Mutex

`utest_prune_records` validates leg pointers inside the exclusive retention mutex, and neither the COUNT of pointers it validates nor the TIME it may spend doing so is bounded. Each pointer costs up to three separate `timeout 5` reads, and the loop cannot stop early: `head -n "$pin_max"` truncates the OUTPUT, but a pointer that fails validation prints nothing, so a directory of invalid pointers is walked to the end. `utest_lease_acquire` waits only 10 seconds for that same mutex and then refuses, so a reader can be denied a lease it was entitled to by a slow or hostile `build/` rather than by anything about its own record.

> [!NOTE]
> Filed 2026-08-02 from §52's design review, rated [medium] there. Pre-existing, and §52 made it measurably worse by adding the third bounded read: worst case per pointer went from ~10s to ~15s. The reviewer's own recommendation -- read the record documents BEFORE taking the mutex, then re-read and re-bind under it -- is rejected here with evidence: the lease-ordering comment above `UTEST_RETENTION_FD` in `scripts/test.sh` records that a read-then-recheck construction "only NARROWS the window ... it buys probability rather than correctness", which is exactly why acquisition holds one mutex from resolving the pointer to publishing its lease. An aggregate DEADLINE keeps that ordering intact and bounds the hazard, so that is the shape to build. -> XREF: `00-infrastructure/TODO-04-usermode-test-framework.md` §52 (item: "`utest_pointer_pin_id` now binds the record to the leg its pointer names")

- [ ] Give the pointer-validation loop an aggregate deadline, not just a per-read timeout, and stop validating once it is spent
      - The budget belongs to the whole loop because the hazard is the SUM: an unbounded number of pointers, each individually bounded, is not a bounded critical section. Pointers already validated keep their pins; the rest simply do not win one this pass, which is the same outcome the `UTEST_POINTER_PIN_MAX` ranking already produces for a loser.
      - Announce it when it bites, the way the lease cap announces itself. A silently truncated validation pass is indistinguishable from a pass that found nothing to pin, and the consequence -- a live generation losing its pin and aging out -- is the one this whole mechanism exists to prevent.
- [ ] Bound the CANDIDATE set before the loop, so a directory full of junk pointers cannot exhaust the budget ahead of the real ones
      - Candidates are already mtime-ordered newest-first, so the natural bound is a multiple of `pin_max` rather than a new tunable nobody sets.
- [ ] Regression: a `build/` holding many invalid pointers still lets a valid one keep its pin, and the pass reports the truncation
      - The existing `uar_prune_run` harness already builds pointer sets; this needs the slow or replaced path as well, which a FIFO at a pointer pathname produces deterministically.
- [ ] Commit: `"test: bound pointer validation under the retention mutex"`

**Test checkpoint:** with many invalid pointers present, a prune pass completes within its aggregate budget, a valid pointer still wins its pin, and any truncated validation is named on stdout rather than silently dropped. Test on: host tooling only (no QEMU dependency).

## 61. Validate the Commit Marker as a Shape, and Read Each Record Document From One Snapshot

Two gaps in `utest_pointer_pin_id` that §52 narrowed without closing. The MARKER is still validated by positive substring tests plus a literal duplicate loop, so a hand-written `{"wrapper": {"schema": ..., "status": "complete", "identity": "test-run-identity.json"}}` -- every required token present, none of them top-level -- satisfies the shell while a real parser finds no keys at all and refuses the generation. And every record document is read with TWO opens (`wc -c` for the size, then `head -c` for the bytes), so a file swapped between them is validated as its predecessor: the size check that exists to defeat a hidden suffix past the read cap does not bind to the inode the read actually returns.

> [!NOTE]
> Filed 2026-08-02 from §52's adversarial round, which rated both [medium]. §52 closed the ESCAPE half of the marker gap outright (no backslash may appear in a marker, so no key can be spelled around the duplicate check) and fixed the parser-equivalence bug it found in the identity document; the nested shape needs the marker's own canonical walk, which is `utest_identity_binds` applied to `utest_commit_record`'s fixed layout and needs the emitter parameterised the same way `utest_identity_doc_for` was. The two-open race was NOT accepted as a §52 blocker and the reasoning is recorded rather than assumed: the shape is identical for the pointer and marker reads that predate §52, `scripts/test.sh` already states the disposition for it ("a replacement arriving while this parse is in flight is still parsed as its predecessor ... the cost of losing that race is seconds and a sweep on the next pass"), and an actor who can win it can also simply write a fully canonical identity naming any leg -- the binding is a mis-resolution check, not an authentication boundary. It is worth closing as ONE change across all three reads, not as a fourth variant. -> XREF: `00-infrastructure/TODO-04-usermode-test-framework.md` §52 (item: "`utest_pointer_pin_id` now binds the record to the leg its pointer names")

- [ ] Parameterise the marker emitter and validate the marker by its canonical shape, the way `utest_identity_binds` validates identity
      - Ten lines, `{`/`}` alone at the ends, eight keys in `utest_commit_record`'s fixed order, each value a scalar or `null`. That rejects the nested wrapper, an extra key and a reordered document in one walk, and it retires the positive-substring plus duplicate-loop pair rather than adding a third layer to it.
      - The fixtures in `scripts/test-tooling.sh` hand-write single-line markers today, so this lands with an emitter they can call -- the same reason `utest_identity_doc_for` and `utest_leg_pointer_doc_for` exist.
- [ ] Read each record document from ONE snapshot, so the size that was checked and the bytes that were validated come from the same inode
      - One time-bounded read of at most cap+1 bytes, refusing on the cap+1st, replaces the `wc`-then-`head` pair for the pointer, the marker and the identity together. Nothing else needs to change: every validator already consumes a snapshot rather than re-opening.
      - It is also the FASTER shape, measured by §52's perf review: 0.38s per 500 reads for a single bounded read against 2.54s for the `wc`+`awk`+`head` pair, i.e. ~6.7ms saved per validated pointer. The catch to design around is that command substitution strips trailing newlines, so the read needs a non-newline sentinel appended to keep the true length observable -- which is the same trap the size-first ordering exists to avoid today.
      - The regression needs to be deterministic, not a sleep race: a FIFO or a replaced pathname between the two opens is reproducible, which is what makes this worth a test rather than a comment. §52 added an `-f` fast path so a STATIC FIFO no longer costs the timeout, but that is a stat and not atomic with the open, so it narrows the window rather than closing it.
- [ ] Make the positive retention fixtures build their markers from that emitter instead of hand-writing a four-field one
      - `uar_prune_reset` writes a one-line marker carrying schema, run_id, status and identity, while production emits eight fields including the payload inventory. The positive pin and lease tests therefore run against records a documented consumer could not open at step 4, and they stay green if production's inventory rules drift. Derive the negative fixtures by mutating the canonical output rather than by spelling a second contract.
- [ ] Commit: `"test: validate the commit marker as a shape and read record documents from one snapshot"`

**Test checkpoint:** a nested or reordered marker carrying every required token wins no pin and no lease; a document replaced between the size check and the read is refused rather than validated as its predecessor; every currently-passing pointer, marker and identity fixture is unaffected. Test on: host tooling only (no QEMU dependency).

## 62. Detaching an Age Candidate Before Classifying It

Retention classifies every record and then destroys the losers in a second loop, and the two are not one operation. Section 53 gave a directory at a lease name an uncapped `D` hold and re-asks the question immediately before `rm -rf`, which narrows the gap to a syscall pair -- but the writer of such a directory is by definition not participating in the retention mutex, so it can still appear inside that pair and be deleted recursively by a run that has already announced such things are never removed. The same gap covers the `U` hold and any future one: a hold is only as good as the distance between the verdict and the destruction.

> [!NOTE]
> Filed 2026-08-02 from section 53's design review, rated [high] by the reviewer and reduced to a narrowed window rather than a closed one in that section. This is the record-level twin of what section 46 did for lease FILES: select by name, then destroy through a detached object nothing else can reach, so a replacement between the check and the action cannot be destroyed in place of what was checked. It is filed separately because it changes the destructive path for EVERY record, not only blocked ones, and a prune killed mid-detach leaves a record under a staging name that needs its own reaper -- exactly the shape section 46 needed a whole section for one level down.

- [ ] Detach an age candidate before classifying it, so the record a hold protects cannot change between the verdict and the `rm -rf`
      - `mv` the record aside into a private retention claim under `RUNS_DIR`, re-take the U/D verdicts on the detached directory, and either restore it or remove it -- only a detached record proven free of blockers is destroyed.
      -> XREF: `00-infrastructure/TODO-04-usermode-test-framework.md` §53 (item: "A blocking directory HOLDS its record, because reporting it while the age cut deleted it was the policy contradicting itself")
- [ ] Reap a detach the previous pass could not finish, without ever removing what it could not classify
      - The staging name must be recognisable and its reap must only ever restore or `rmdir`, for the reason `utest_sweep_reap` only ever `rmdir`s: a claim holding an unresolved object is reported and kept, never deleted.
- [ ] Cover the race directly: a blocker created after classification and before destruction survives
      - The existing L26b proves the hold works when the blocker is present at classification time; this needs a fixture that creates it in the window, which the section-46 tests already do by wrapping the production verdict function.
- [ ] Commit: `"test: destroy an aged record through a detached object, not through its published name"`

**Test checkpoint:** a record whose blocker appears between classification and destruction survives with its contents intact; a detach interrupted mid-prune is restored or reported on the next pass and never silently dropped; every currently-passing retention fixture is unaffected. Test on: host tooling only (no QEMU dependency).

> **Parity state (§1-§17 shipped):** every 💎 parity row is ✅ except `Perf regression`, which is ⚠️ because §8 asserts against hardcoded thresholds rather than tracking a historical baseline over time -- threshold regressions fail the run, but silent drift below the threshold would not. Closing that to full ✅ needs launcher-side `tests/perf-baseline.json` drift detection, which is deferred to §8 follow-up pending an env-passing syscall (tracked inline in §8's Deferred stamp). **⭐ exclusives:** §5 fault-inject bridge gives a typed `test=1`-gated kernel-allocator probe surface that AppVerifier hooks Win32 for and Linux only exposes through debugfs; §14 Win32-on-non-Win depends on `D02T12 §6` Win32 thunk landing; §17 closes the fast-path stability floor (probes + ABI generator + invariant panic) so TEB/KUSD/syscall transports are as robust as Win11 TEB reads and Linux vDSO calls; §18 will put Impossible OS past both competitors by baking versioned ABI handshake, self-describing KUSD, always-on ring-3 invariants, always-on transition ring-buffer dumps, and in-boot transport fuzzing into the baseline kernel -- capabilities that on Win11 and Linux require opt-in profilers, external fuzzers, or debug-only builds. **❓ = competitor cell UNVERIFIED.** The two §24 artifact rows carry ❓ because the parity research for them could not be sourced: the researcher's `WebSearch`/`WebFetch` were blocked for the whole dispatch, so the TRX and kselftest/TAP claims are recollection, not citation. The Impossible OS column is measured from the shipped artifacts and stands; re-verifying the competitor cells (and deciding whether the utest JSON schema should align with a published cross-tool schema) is owned by §27. §27 could NOT clear them either: web access was blocked for a second consecutive dispatch, this time with the cause identified (the R4 headless reroute blocks the researcher agent it reroutes TO, so both ends are blocked -- filed against the runner TODO). The two ❓ rows §27 adds carry it for the same reason. §36's `Per-binary captured stdout` row carries ❓ for the SAME web-access reason as §24/§27's -- the Impossible OS cell is measured (17/17 binaries, byte-identical across both artifacts, reconciled fail-closed), and it is marked ⭐ rather than 💎 because neither competitor reconciles captured bytes against a producer-declared count at all: they carry whatever the harness scraped. §37's two rows carry ❓ for the THIRD-CONSECUTIVE-BLOCK reason: its review DID dispatch `parity-research-analyst`, but `websearch_offload_gate.py` rule R4 blocked both `WebSearch` and `WebFetch` for the researcher agent it reroutes TO, so the TRX outcome vocabulary and the TAP claim remain recollection rather than citation -- the Impossible OS cells are measured (984 tooling assertions, both assemblers reconciled fail-closed, `binaries_reported` reconciled against the records carrying a triple) and the competitor cells are what re-verification would change. The analyst's unblocked, code-read findings were acted on: they are §44's JSON-abort-record item and the reason-lint item. §34's `Orphaned-VM recovery` row carries ❓ on a DIFFERENT ground: HLK and kselftest do not launch the machine their tests run on, so there is no published orphan-reaping contract to compare against rather than a claim that went unsourced -- calling that ❌ would assert an absence in a harness the question does not apply to. §45's `Reader lease` row carries ❓ on the same footing, and deliberately NOT ❌: "HLK and kselftest have no reader lease" is an ABSENCE claim about a competitor, which is exactly the class that needs a citation rather than recollection, and no web dispatch was spent to get one. The Impossible OS cell is measured (21 tooling regressions covering hold, reclaim, expiry, the TTL ceiling and both its evasions, admission, contention, malformed leases, record/filename binding, and every liveness verdict). §52's row carries ❓ on exactly §45's footing and for the same reason -- neither competitor publishes a per-leg generation pointer, so "their pointer is not bound to its producing leg" is an absence claim about a thing that does not exist there, which needs a citation rather than recollection, and no web dispatch was spent to get one. §53's row carries ❓ on that same footing once more: "HLK and kselftest have no policy for a directory standing at a result pathname" is an absence claim, and the honest reading is that the question is not asked there rather than that it is answered badly -- both harnesses would surface it as an ordinary write failure, which is a mechanism, not a policy. The Impossible OS cell is measured (the two namespaces share one verdict function, four regressions pin report / hold / age-out / the `UTEST_LEASE_MAX=0` boundary, and one pins that nothing a healthy run writes takes a hold). §58's two rows carry ❓ on §34's footing rather than §45's: HLK and kselftest are HOST processes whose timeouts are measured by an OS whose clock stopping would take the harness with it, so "they have no bound independent of the tick" describes a question that is not asked there rather than one answered badly -- the in-kernel launcher is the only one of the three that can outlive its own timekeeping. The Impossible OS cells are measured (ten regressions over a synthetic clock pair: watchdog escape, iteration-ceiling escape, no-watchdog-source escape, a coarse clock that must NOT be called a stall, and the reap's stall-stickiness and withheld free). Everything the ❓ cells would change is a competitor claim, never an Impossible OS one.

## Unit Tests

> [!NOTE]
> User-mode coverage is driven by `user/test/test_*.c` binaries and serial `[UTEST]` lines from §3 onward, not a dedicated `src/kernel/test/test_usermode.c` until a kernel-side wrapper is justified. §2 is header-only parity; §4 + §7 + §8 are launcher and serial-format policy. The §5 `SYS_FAULT_INJECT` test-mode gate negative regression is the one kernel-side `TEST_CAT_EXEC` assertion that DOES belong in `src/kernel/test/test_syscall.c`.

- [x] Commit: `"test: N/A single TEST_CAT file -- usermode harness per §1-§16 and Verification"` (no new kernel test file; user-mode coverage IS the test surface per the NOTE above, plus the single §5 SYS_FAULT_INJECT regression in test_syscall.c which shipped under its section)

**Test checkpoint:** After §3 ships, `bash scripts/test.sh SUITE=exec` (see `CLAUDE.md`) parses `[UTEST]` PASS/FAIL alongside kernel `[TEST]` lines.

## Verification

- [x] `make test` -> user-mode tests run after kernel tests and all pass (KVM 2026-04-23: `bash scripts/test.sh QUIET=1` exit=0, summary `PASS: 2184 kernel + 16 user-mode tests passed`; TCG 2026-04-23 via `FORCE_TCG=1`: 2166 kernel + 16 user-mode PASS, 0 leaked)
- [x] Break a syscall -> a user-mode test catches it -> `bash scripts/test.sh` fails locally. Demonstrated 2026-04-23: injected `ret = -1;` after the legitimate `ret = t->pid` in `src/kernel/sched/syscall.c` `case SYS_GETPID`, rebuilt + ran `bash scripts/test.sh QUIET=1`. Result: `test_fastpath.exe FAIL (exit=4)` (probe 3: `gs:0x40 ClientId.UniqueProcess` did not match `sys_getpid`) + `test_fastpath_fuzz.exe FAIL (exit=1)` (`sys_getpid baseline is positive` assertion fired) + wrapper exit=1 + `FAIL: 0 kernel + 2 user-mode of 2200 failed`. Reverted in the same session; subsequent run returned `exit=0` + `PASS: 2184 kernel + 16 user-mode tests passed`.
- [x] Add a new test binary -> one file + one Makefile line -> works in `make test` / `bash scripts/test.sh`. Demonstrated by the existing 16 binaries under `user/test/` (`test_smoke_boot`, `test_fastpath`, `test_fastpath_fuzz`, `test_harness_smoke`, `test_syscall`, `test_libc`, `test_ipc`, `test_process`, `test_fileio`, `test_win32`, `test_loader_elf`, `test_loader_pe`, `test_loader_eif`, `test_faultinject`, `test_stress_libc`, `test_perf_syscall`) -- each landed via the §16 build pattern (one `user/test/test_<name>.c` + one `USERMODE_TESTS` row in [`Makefile`](../../Makefile) `userland` target + one `tests/usermode.manifest` line). The launcher's VFS scan + manifest dispatch picks them up automatically; the §16 commit history (`f0279dc0`, `86a4edf4`, `8a5eced9`, `9054b594`, `f8d6f9d8`) is the historical workflow proof.
- [x] Commit: `"test: user-mode test framework complete"`

**Test checkpoint:** End to end: clean tree -> `bash scripts/test.sh` is green -> a one-line change breaks a `test_*.exe` assertion -> the run fails with a visible `[UTEST] FAIL`. Demonstrated 2026-04-23 via the SYS_GETPID break above. Test on: QEMU WHPX, QEMU TCG (closed via `FORCE_TCG=1` 2026-04-23), VirtualBox, bare metal.

**Test runner:** `bash scripts/test.sh` | 2184 kernel + 16 user-mode PASS on KVM 2026-04-23 (exit=0); 2166 + 16 PASS on TCG via `FORCE_TCG=1`; per-binary bat files under `scripts/debug/usermode/` for targeted runs.
